We can solve this by:
1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point i, we can use binary search or DP to find the longest such subset

But there's a simpler greedy approach: we can use the fact that any clique corresponds to a set where all x_j - w_j are ≥ all x_i + w_i.

So we can:
- Sort by x
- For each possible value of max(x_i + w_i), find how many points have x_j - w_j ≥ that value
- Take the maximum

Actually, the most efficient approach:
- Sort points by x-coordinate
- For each point, compute A_i = x_i + w_i and B_i = x_i - w_i
- The condition for a clique is that min(B_j) ≥ max(A_i) for all i,j in the clique
- This means we can iterate through all possible values of t where t = max(A_i) for some clique, and find how many points have B_j ≥ t
- The answer is the maximum such count

But we can do better: we can consider that in a valid clique, if we let M = max(x_i + w_i), then all points in the clique must have x_j - w_j ≥ M.

So for a fixed M, the number of points satisfying x_j - w_j ≥ M is the size of the clique when M is the maximum of x_i + w_i.

We can iterate through all possible values of M (which are the A_i values), and find the maximum count.

Steps:
1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. Compute A_i = x_i + w_i, B_i = x_i - w_i for each point
3. For each possible A_k, consider M = A_k, and count how many points j have B_j ≥ A_k
4. The answer is the maximum count over all k

But since we want the maximum clique, and every clique has some maximum A_i value, this should work.

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# Calculate A_i = x_i + w_i and B_i = x_i - w_i
A = []
B = []
for x, w in points:
    A.append(x + w)
    B.append(x - w)

# For each possible maximum A value, count how many B values are >= that A value
max_clique = 0

# We consider each A value as the maximum (x_i + w_i) in the clique
for i in range(n):
    threshold = A[i]
    count = 0
    for j in range(n):
        if B[j] >= threshold:
            count += 1
    max_clique = max(max_clique, count)

print(max_clique)
```

But this is O(n^2). For n = 200,000, that's too slow.

We need to optimize. After sorting by x, when we fix threshold = A[i], we want to count how many B[j] >= A[i].

Since both A and B arrays are sorted by x (which is the same as the point order), we can use binary search on B.

Actually, the B array is not necessarily sorted. But we have points sorted by x, so the B array is just the values x_j - w_j for points in order.

To optimize, we can:
1. Sort points by x
2. Precompute A and B arrays
3. For each A[i], we want count of j such that B[j] >= A[i]
4. Since B array is in the same order as x, we can use binary search to find the position where B[j] >= A[i]

But binary search over the entire B array for each i is still O(n log n).

Actually, we can reverse the process. The maximum clique is at most the number of points, and we can try to use a different approach.

After checking known problems, I recall that the maximum clique in this specific graph corresponds to the longest sequence where the values of (x_i - w_i) are non-decreasing with respect to the values of (x_j + w_j).

But let me implement the O(n log n) solution:

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# Calculate A_i = x_i + w_i and B_i = x_i - w_i
A = []
B = []
for x, w in points:
    A.append(x + w)
    B.append(x - w)

# Count for each A[i] how many B[j] >= A[i]
max_clique = 0

# Sort B in descending order to make binary search easier
B.sort(reverse=True)

# For each A[i], find the count of B[j] >= A[i]
# Since B is sorted in descending order, we can use binary search
for a_val in A:
    # Find the first position where B[pos] >= a_val
    # Since B is sorted descending, we want the leftmost position where B[pos] >= a_val
    import bisect
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(B, a_val)
    count = len(B) - pos
    max_clique = max(max_clique, count)

print(max_clique)
```

Wait, this won't work because B is sorted in descending order, but we're losing the coordinate ordering.

Actually, when we sort B in descending order, we're not preserving the relationship to the original indices. But we don't need to - we just need to count how many values in B are >= a_val.

And since we're iterating through all possible thresholds (A[i]), this should work.

But actually, the B values in the sorted array are from all original points, not in the same order as the original.

Yes, we just need to count how many points have B[j] >= A[i].

So the code is:

```python
import bisect

n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# Calculate A_i = x_i + w_i and B_i = x_i - w_i
A = []
B = []
for x, w in points:
    A.append(x + w)
    B.append(x - w)

# Count for each A[i] how many B[j] >= A[i]
max_clique = 0

# Sort B in descending order (or just use bisect)
# We want the first index where B[pos] >= a_val
# Since we want >=, we use bisect_left on the sorted array
B.sort(reverse=True)  # now B is sorted descending

for a_val in A:
    # Find the first position where value >= a_val
    # In descending order, this is the leftmost position
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(B, a_val)
    count = len(B) - pos
    max_clique = max(max_clique, count)

print(max_clique)
```

But I need to be more careful. When I sort B, I'm sorting all values, which is fine because we just need to count how many values are >= a_val.

This should work, but let me verify with the example:

Input: 4 points
(2,3), (3,1), (6,1), (0,2)

Sort by x: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

A = [0+2, 2+3, 3+1, 6+1] = [2, 5, 4, 7]
B = [0-2, 2-3, 3-1, 6-1] = [-2, -1, 2, 5]

Sort B descending: [5, 2, -1, -2]

For A[0]=2: find first position where B[pos] >= 2 → pos=1 (B[1]=2), count=4-1=3
For A[1]=5: find first pos where B[pos] >= 5 → pos=0 (B[0]=5), count=4-0=4
For A[2]=4: first pos where >=4 → pos=0 (B[0]=5>=4), count=4
For A[3]=7: first pos where >=7 → pos=4 (not exist), count=0

Maximum count is 4.

But expected output is 3.

This suggests my reasoning is wrong.

Let me check the edge conditions manually.

Points: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

Check if all pairs satisfy |x_i-x_j| >= w_i + w_j:

(0,2) and (2,3): |0-2|=2, w1+w2=2+3=5, 2 < 5 → no edge
(0,2) and (3,1): |0-3|=3, w1+w2=2+1=3, 3 >= 3 → edge
(0,2) and (6,1): |0-6|=6, w1+w2=2+1=3, 6 >= 3 → edge

(2,3) and (3,1): |2-3|=1, w1+w2=3+1=4, 1 < 4 → no edge
(2,3) and (6,1): |2-6|=4, w1+w2=3+1=4, 4 >= 4 → edge
(3,1) and (6,1): |3-6|=3, w1+w2=1+1=2, 3 >= 2 → edge

So edges exist for:
(0,2)-(3,1), (0,2)-(6,1), (2,3)-(6,1), (3,1)-(6,1)

Now check possible cliques:
- {(0,2), (3,1), (6,1)}: check all pairs:
  - (0,2)-(3,1): yes
  - (0,2)-(6,1): yes  
  - (3,1)-(6,1): yes
  → valid, size 3

- {(2,3), (6,1)}: yes, size 2

- {(0,2), (6,1)}: yes, size 2

So maximum clique size is 3.

My algorithm gave 4, which is wrong.

Where did I go wrong?

The issue is that my condition "B_j >= A_i" is necessary but not sufficient? Or I misunderstood.

For points i and j with x_i < x_j, the edge condition is |x_i - x_j| >= w_i + w_j, which is x_j - x_i >= w_i + w_j.

This is equivalent to x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.

So for points i and j with x_i < x_j, they have an edge if and only if (x_j - w_j) >= (x_i + w_i).

In a clique, for any two points i < j in the clique, we need (x_j - w_j) >= (x_i + w_i).

So if we have a set of points in order of increasing x, then for any two i < j in the set, (x_j - w_j) >= (x_i + w_i).

This means that the values of (x_i + w_i) must be less than or equal to the values of (x_j - w_j) for all later points.

For the entire clique, if we let M = max_{k in clique} (x_k + w_k), then for every point j in the clique, we need (x_j - w_j) >= M.

Because for any point i in the clique, (x_j - w_j) >= (x_i + w_i) for all j > i, and since M is the maximum of (x_i + w_i), we need (x_j - w_j) >= M.

Actually, for any point j in the clique, and any point i in the clique (i < j), we need (x_j - w_j) >= (x_i + w_i).

Since M is the maximum of (x_i + w_i) over all i in the clique, we need (x_j - w_j) >= M.

But this must hold for every j in the clique.

So for a valid clique, we need min_{j in clique} (x_j - w_j) >= max_{i in clique} (x_i + w_i).

Yes! This is the correct condition.

So the algorithm should be:
1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each possible maximum value of (x_i + w_i) in the clique, say M, we need to find the maximum size of a subset where min(x_j - w_j) >= M
3. This is equivalent to: for each possible M = A[i] for some i, count the number of points j such that (x_j - w_j) >= M, and also ensure that M is achievable as max(x_i + w_i)

Since the points are sorted, for a fixed M, we can count how many points have (x_j - w_j) >= M.

But we also need that the maximum of (x_i + w_i) in the subset is exactly M.

However, if we have a subset where min(x_j - w_j) >= M, then max(x_i + w_i) could be less than or equal to M.

Actually, for the subset to have maximum (x_i + w_i) = M, we need that at least one point has x_i + w_i = M, and all others have x_i + w_i <= M.

But since we want the maximum clique size over all possible M, and every clique has some maximum of (x_i + w_i), we can consider all possible values of M that appear in A.

So the algorithm is:
- Sort points by x
- For each i, let M = A[i] = x_i + w_i
- Count the number of points j such that x_j - w_j >= M
- The answer is the maximum count over all i

But in the example:
A = [2,5,4,7]
B = [-2,-1,2,5]

For M=2: count points with x_j - w_j >= 2 → (3,1):2, (6,1):5 → count=2
For M=5: count points with x_j - w_j >= 5 → (6,1):5 → count=1  
For M=4: count points with x_j - w_j >= 4 → (6,1):5 → count=1
For M=7: count points with x_j - w_j >= 7 → 0 → count=0

Maximum is 2, not 3.

Still wrong.

I see the issue: I need to include points where x_j - w_j >= some value M, and M is the maximum of (x_i + w_i) in the subset.

But in the clique {(0,2), (3,1), (6,1)}, the values are:
- (0,2): x_i + w_i = 2, x_j - w_j = -2
- (3,1): x_i + w_i = 4, x_j - w_j = 2
- (6,1): x_i + w_i = 7, x_j - w_j = 5

So max(x_i + w_i) = 7, min(x_j - w_j) = 2.

We need min(x_j - w_j) >= max(x_i + w_i) → 2 >= 7? False.

This suggests my condition is backwards.

Let me re-derive:

For two points i < j with x_i < x_j, they have an edge if x_j - x_i >= w_i + w_j.

This is equivalent to x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.

For a clique of points in order of increasing x, we need for every pair i < j: x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.

This means that the sequence of values (x_i + w_i) must be such that each value is <= the value of (x_j - w_j) for all j > i.

This is equivalent to: for any two points i < j in the clique, (x_j - w_j) >= (x_i + w_i).

This means that if we have a sequence of values a_i = x_i + w_i, b_i = x_i - w_i, then for any i < j, b_j >= a_i.

This is equivalent to: the minimum of b_j for j in the clique >= the maximum of a_i for i in the clique.

Yes! min_{j in clique} (x_j - w_j) >= max_{i in clique} (x_i + w_i)

In the example: 
Clique {(0,2), (3,1), (6,1)}:
- a_i values: 2, 4, 7 → max = 7
- b_j values: -2, 2, 5 → min = -2
- -2 >= 7? False

But we know the clique is valid.

Check the edge between (0,2) and (3,1): |0-3|=3, w1+w2=2+1=3, 3>=3 → edge
(0,2) and (6,1): |0-6|=6, w1+w2=2+1=3, 6>=3 → edge
(3,1) and (6,1): |3-6|=3, w1+w2=1+1=2, 3>=2 → edge

All edges exist, so it should be a clique.

The condition for (0,2) and (3,1): x_3 - w_3 >= x_0 + w_0 → 3-1 >= 0+2 → 2 >= 2 → true
For (0,2) and (6,1): 6-1 >= 0+2 → 5 >= 2 → true
For (3,1) and (6,1): 6-1 >= 3+1 → 5 >= 4 → true

So the condition b_j >= a_i for i < j is correct.

In the clique, for any i < j, b_j >= a_i.

This does not imply that min b_j >= max a_i.

For example in the clique, a_0=2, a_1=4, a_2=7; b_0=-2, b_1=2, b_2=5
For i=0, j=1: b_1=2 >= a_0=2 ✓
For i=0, j=2: b_2=5 >= a_0=2 ✓
For i=1, j=2: b_2=5 >= a_1=4 ✓

So the pairwise condition is satisfied.

The condition min b_j >= max a_i is sufficient but not necessary.

We need a different approach.

I recall that in such problems, the maximum clique can be found by dynamic programming or by considering that the graph is a "unit disk" graph or has a known structure.

After research, I recall that for this problem, the maximum clique is the longest sequence where for every pair i < j, x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.

This is equivalent to: if we define a_i = x_i + w_i, then for every pair i < j in the sequence, a_j <= (x_j - w_j) + something.

Wait, the condition is x_j - w_j >= a_i.

So for a sequence of points in order of increasing x, we need that for every i < j, a_i <= x_j - w_j.

This means that if we let c_j = x_j - w_j, then for every i < j, a_i <= c_j.

So the values a_i must be <= c_j for all j > i.

This is equivalent to: the maximum of a_i over i < j must be <= c_j.

So if we process points in order of increasing x, we can maintain the maximum of a_i seen so far, and for each point j, if c_j >= max_seen, then we can extend the sequence.

The maximum clique size is the longest such subsequence.

Algorithm:
1. Sort points by x
2. Let a_i = x_i + w_i, c_i = x_i - w_i
3. Use greedy: initialize size = 1, max_a = 0
4. For each point in sorted order:
   - If c_i >= max_a, then we can include this point, new_max_a = max(max_a, a_i)
   - Otherwise, we cannot include it
5. But is this greedy correct?

Let me test with example:
Points sorted by x: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)
a = [2,5,4,7]
c = [-2,-1,2,5]

i=0: c0=-2, max_a=0, -2 < 0 → cannot include? size=1
i=1: c1=-1, max_a=0, -1 < 0 → cannot include
i=2: c2=2, max_a=0, 2 >= 0 → can include, new_max_a = max(0,4)=4
i=3: c3=5, max_a=4, 5 >= 4 → can include, new_max_a = max(4,7)=7

Size = 3 (points 2 and 3), but we only have 2 points.

The points are: after including point 2 (3,1) and point 3 (6,1), size=2.

We need to count the number of points in the sequence.

The greedy algorithm should be: we maintain the maximum a_i among the selected points. For each new point, if c_i >= current_max_a, we can select it and update current_max_a = max(current_max_a, a_i).

For the example:
- Start: select nothing, max_a = -inf or 0
- Point (0,2): c0=-2, max_a=0, -2 < 0 → skip
- Point (2,3): c1=-1, max_a=0, -1 < 0 → skip
- Point (3,1): c2=2, max_a=0, 2 >= 0 → select, max_a = max(0,4) = 4
- Point (6,1): c3=5, max_a=4, 5 >= 4 → select, max_a = max(4,7) = 7

Size = 2, but expected is 3.

We missed the point (0,2) and (3,1) and (6,1).

The point (0,2) has c0=-2, a0=2.

If we select (0,2) first: max_a = 2, then c0=-2 < 2, so we cannot add any point with c_i < 2.

We can only select (0,2) if we don't add any other point.

So the only options are:
- size 1: any single point
- size 2: (3,1) and (6,1) as above
- size 3: (0,2), (3,1), (6,1) — but for (0,2) and (3,1): c1=2 >= a0=2 ✓, for (0,2) and (6,1): c3=5 >= a0=2 ✓, for (3,1) and (6,1): c3=5 >= a1=4 ✓

So it should be allowed.

The condition is that for every pair i < j in the selected set, c_j >= a_i.

This is not the same as c_j >= max_{k in selected} a_k.

In the example with (0,2), (3,1), (6,1):
- For (0,2) and (3,1): j=1, c1=2 >= a0=2 ✓
- For (0,2) and (6,1): j=3, c3=5 >= a0=2 ✓
- For (3,1) and (6,1): j=3, c3=5 >= a1=4 ✓

So the condition is that for each j, c_j >= a_i for all i < j.

This is equivalent to: for each j, c_j >= max_{i < j, i in selected} a_i.

So we can do:

1. Sort by x
2. Let size = 0
3. For each point j in order:
   - Let current_max_a = max of a_i for i in selected and i < j
   - If c_j >= current_max_a, then we can add point j, and update current_max_a = max(current_max_a, a_j)

But when we add point j, we need to know the max of a_i for i in selected with i < j.

This can be done with a variable that keeps track of the maximum a_i among selected points up to now.

We can do a dynamic programming or greedy: at each step, if the current point's c_j is at least the current maximum a_i among selected points, we can include it.

In the example:
- j=0: selected={}, max_a = -inf, c0=-2 >= -inf → include, max_a = max(-inf,2)=2
- j=1: c1=-1, max_a=2, -1 >= 2? False → skip
- j=2: c2=2, max_a=2, 2 >= 2 → include, max_a = max(2,4)=4
- j=3: c3=5, max_a=4, 5 >= 4 → include, max_a = max(4,7)=7

Size = 3. This matches!

Let me verify the condition for each pair:
- (0,2) and (3,1): c1=2 >= a0=2 ✓
- (0,2) and (6,1): c3=5 >= a0=2 ✓
- (3,1) and (6,1): c3=5 >= a1=4 ✓

Yes.

Another example: only two points (0,2) and (2,3)
a = [2,5], c = [-2,-1]
- j=0: include, max_a=2
- j=1: c1=-1, max_a=2, -1 >= 2? False → size=1

Edges: |0-2|=2, w1+w2=2+3=5, 2<5 → no edge, so size 1 is correct.

Another example: (0,2) and (3,1)
- j=0: include, max_a=2
- j=1: c1=2, max_a=2, 2 >= 2 → include, max_a=4
Size=2
Edges: |0-3|=3, w1+w2=2+1=3, 3>=3 → edge, so size 2 is correct.

So the algorithm is:

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

# Sort by coordinate
points.sort()

# For each point, calculate a_i = x_i + w_i and c_i = x_i - w_i
a = []
c = []
for x, w in points:
    a.append(x + w)
    c.append(x - w)

# Greedy algorithm
max_a = -10**18  # a very small number
size = 0

for i in range(n):
    if c[i] >= max_a:
        size += 1
        max_a = max(max_a, a[i])

print(size)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The clique problem is one of the most well-known NP-complete problems. Under some simplification it can be formulated as follows. Consider an undirected graph G. It is required to find a subset of vertices C of the maximum size such that any two of them are connected by an edge in graph G. Sounds simple, doesn't it? Nobody yet knows an algorithm that finds a solution to this problem in polynomial time of the size of the graph. However, as with many other NP-complete problems, the clique problem is easier if you consider a specific type of a graph.

Consider n distinct points on a line. Let the i-th point have the coordinate x_{i} and weight w_{i}. Let's form graph G, whose vertices are these points and edges connect exactly the pairs of points (i, j), such that the distance between them is not less than the sum of their weights, or more formally: |x_{i} - x_{j}| ≥ w_{i} + w_{j}.

Find the size of the maximum clique in such graph.


-----Input-----

The first line contains the integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 200 000) — the number of points.

Each of the next n lines contains two numbers x_{i}, w_{i} (0 ≤ x_{i} ≤ 10^9, 1 ≤ w_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the coordinate and the weight of a point. All x_{i} are different.


-----Output-----

Print a single number — the number of vertexes in the maximum clique of the given graph.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
2 3
3 1
6 1
0 2

Output
3

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have n points on a line with coordinates x_i and weights w_i
2. We form a graph where vertices are these points
3. There's an edge between points i and j if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j
4. We need to find the maximum clique size (largest subset where every pair is connected)

## Key Insight

The key observation is that we need to find the maximum clique in this specific graph. Let's think about when two points are **not** connected (i.e., when they DON'T have an edge).

Two points i and j are NOT connected when: |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j

Since the points are on a line, we can assume x_i ≤ x_j without loss of generality, so this becomes: x_j - x_i < w_i + w_j
Which simplifies to: x_j - w_j < x_i + w_i

This means that if two points are "close" in the transformed space (where we consider x_i + w_i and x_j - w_j), they don't have an edge.

## Transformation Approach

For any two points i and j, they don't have an edge if and only if:
- x_j - w_j < x_i + w_i OR x_i - w_i < x_j + w_j

Actually, let me reorganize: if we have points with coordinates x and weights w, then points i and j have NO edge when |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

This is equivalent to: x_j - x_i < w_i + w_j (when x_i ≤ x_j)

Rearranging: x_j - w_j < x_i + w_i

So if we define for each point i: 
- left_i = x_i - w_i
- right_i = x_i + w_i

Then points i and j have NO edge if and only if right_i > left_j AND right_j > left_i (in the case where x_i ≤ x_j).

Actually, let me think differently. Two points i and j have an edge if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.

This means they don't have an edge if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

So if we sort the points by coordinate, then for any two points i and j (i < j), they don't have an edge if x_j - x_i < w_i + w_j.

This is equivalent to: x_j - w_j < x_i + w_i.

Wait, let me check: x_j - x_i < w_i + w_j
→ x_j - w_j < x_i + w_i ✓

So points i and j don't have an edge if and only if x_i + w_i > x_j - w_j.

## Maximum Clique Insight

In a graph where edges exist when |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j, the absence of edges occurs when points are "close" in a transformed space.

The key insight is that in such a graph, the maximum clique corresponds to a set of points where NO two points are "close" in the transformed coordinate system.

But actually, let me think about the complement: if we have a set of points where for every pair, they are far enough apart (have an edge), then they form a clique.

However, I recall that for this specific type of problem with points on a line, the maximum clique can be found by considering intervals.

Let me think about the transformed coordinates:
- For each point i, define L_i = x_i - w_i and R_i = x_i + w_i

Two points i and j have an edge if |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j.
This means they don't have an edge if |x_i - x_j| < w_i + w_j.

For points with x_i ≤ x_j: x_j - x_i < w_i + w_j → x_j - w_j < x_i + w_i.

So points i and j don't have an edge if x_j - w_j < x_i + w_i.

This means if we consider the values x_i + w_i and x_j - w_j, then non-edges occur when one is less than the other.

## Final Approach

I believe the maximum clique corresponds to a set of points where for any two points i and j, we have x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

After researching the standard approach for this type of problem, I recall that:

1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point, consider its "critical region"
3. The maximum clique size is essentially finding the longest subsequence where each consecutive pair satisfies the edge condition

Actually, let me implement a simpler approach:

The maximum clique is actually determined by the property that in the transformed space, if we look at the values x_i - w_i and x_i + w_i, then for any two points to be connected, they should be "far enough".

The standard solution for this problem is:
1. Sort points by x-coordinate
2. For each point, we can binary search to find the maximum clique ending at that point
3. The condition for two points to have an edge is |x_i - x_j| ≥ w_i + w_j

But after careful thought, I believe the maximum clique can be found by considering that points that are "too close" don't connect. The maximum clique size is actually found by looking for the longest sequence where each consecutive pair has distance ≥ sum of weights.

Looking at the sample:
- Point 1: (2,3)
- Point 2: (3,1) 
- Point 3: (6,1)
- Point 4: (0,2)

After sorting by x: (0,2), (2,3), (3,1), (6,1)

Check edges:
- (0,2) and (2,3): |0-2| = 2, w1+w2 = 2+3 = 5 → 2 < 5 → NO edge
- (0,2) and (3,1): |0-3| = 3, w1+w2 = 2+1 = 3 → 3 = 3 → edge (≥)
- (0,2) and (6,1): |0-6| = 6, w1+w2 = 2+1 = 3 → 6 ≥ 3 → edge

- (2,3) and (3,1): |2-3| = 1, w1+w2 = 3+1 = 4 → 1 < 4 → NO edge
- (2,3) and (6,1): |2-6| = 4, w1+w2 = 3+1 = 4 → 4 ≥ 4 → edge

- (3,1) and (6,1): |3-6| = 3, w1+w2 = 1+1 = 2 → 3 ≥ 2 → edge

So edges exist between: (0,2)-(3,1), (0,2)-(6,1), (2,3)-(6,1), (3,1)-(6,1)

The maximum clique might be {(0,2), (3,1), (6,1)} which has size 3.

The approach is to sort by x-coordinate, then use dynamic programming or greedy method.

Standard solution for this problem is to use the fact that the maximum clique size equals the size of the largest subset where for every pair (i,j), x_j - x_i ≥ w_i + w_j.

I'll implement a solution using coordinate transformation:

    # For (3,1) to (6,1): 3 >= 1+1=2 → yes
    
    # For (0,2) and (3,1): x_j - w_j = 3-1=2, x_i + w_i = 0+2=2 → 2 >= 2 → yes
    # So the condition x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i holds for both (0,2) and (3,1), and for (0,2) and (6,1): 6-1=5 >= 0+2=2 → yes
    # For (3,1) and (6,1): 6-1=5 >= 3+1=4 → yes
    
    # So the condition should be that for any two points i < j in sorted order, x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.
    
    # In the set {(0,2), (3,1), (6,1)}:
    # (0,2) and (3,1): 2 >= 2 → yes
    # (0,2) and (6,1): 5 >= 2 → yes
    # (3,1) and (6,1): 5 >= 4 → yes
    # So it should be included.
    
    # But in our algorithm, when we process (0,2), we have current_max_L = 0, and x_j - w_j = -2 < 0, so we skip it.
    
    # The issue is that our current_max_L should be the maximum of (x_i + w_i) among the points in the clique, 
    # and we are checking if x_j - w_j >= current_max_L.
    
    # When we process (0,2), current_max_L should be 0 (initial), and -2 >= 0 is false.
    
    # But (0,2) should be included with (3,1) and (6,1) because for any point in the clique, 
    # its (x - w) >= (x_i + w_i) for the other point.
    
    # For the pair (0,2) and (3,1): we need x_3 - w_3 >= x_0 + w_0 → 3-1=2 >= 0+2=2 → yes
    # For (0,2) and (6,1): 6-1=5 >= 0+2=2 → yes
    # For (3,1) and (6,1): 6-1=5 >= 3+1=4 → yes
    
    # So the condition is that for every pair (i,j) in the set, x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.
    # This is equivalent to: for the entire set, min(x_i - w_i) >= max(x_j + w_j) is false.
    # We need min(x_i - w_i) >= max(x_j + w_j) for the set to be a clique? 
    # No, because if min(x_i - w_i) >= max(x_j + w_j), then for any i,j: x_i - w_i >= x_j + w_j, 
    # which means x_i - w_i >= x_j + w_j, but we only need x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i for i < j.
    
    # After more thought, I believe the correct condition for a set to be a clique is:
    # For every pair (i,j), x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i (when x_i <= x_j)
    # This is equivalent to: for the entire set, when sorted, for each j, x_j - w_j >= max_{i < j} (x_i + w_i)
    
    # But to include (0,2) in the clique with (3,1) and (6,1), we need to have when we process (0,2):
    # x_0 - w_0 >= max of (x_i + w_i) for i in the clique that will be processed later.
    
    # This suggests we should process in order of x, and for each point, if it can extend the current chain (i.e., x_j - w_j >= max of (x_i + w_i) for i with x_i <= x_j), then include it.
    
    # But we can do it in any order. The condition is that for any i < j in sorted order, x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i.
    
    # So we can process in sorted order by x, and for each point, if x_j - w_j >= current_max_L, then we can include it.
    # The current_max_L should be the maximum of (x_i + w_i) for all points i that are in the clique and have x_i <= x_j.
    
    # In the example:
    # j=0: (0,2), current_max_L = 0, x_j - w_j = -2 >= 0? No
    # j=1: (2,3), current_max_L = 0, x_j - w_j = -1 >= 0? No
    # j=2: (3,1), current_max_L = 2 (from point 0), x_j - w_j = 2 >= 2? Yes → include, current_max_L = max(2,4) = 4
    # j=3: (6,1), current_max_L = 4, x_j - w_j = 5 >= 4? Yes → include, current_max_L = max(4,7) = 7
    # Size = 2
    
    # But we want to include (0,2) in the clique with (3,1) and (6,1).
    # The condition for (0,2) and (3,1): 2 >= 2 → yes
    # The condition for (0,2) and (6,1): 5 >= 2 → yes
    # So (0,2) should be included.
    
    # The issue is that when we process (0,2), we only compare with the current_max_L which is 0, not with the future points.
    
    # We need to include a point only if it can satisfy the condition with ALL previously included points, not just the ones with x_i < x_j.
    
    # This is not possible with a simple chain. We need to try all subsets.
    
    # Given the complexity, I'll implement the known solution for this exact problem.

After research, the correct solution is:

```python
n = int(input())
points = []
for _ in range(n):
    x, w = map(int, input().split())
    points.append((x, w))

points.sort()

# The maximum clique size is the maximum number of points such that for any two points in the subset,
# the distance between them is at least the sum of their weights.

# Sort by x-coordinate
points.sort()

# We will use a different approach: the maximum clique size can be found by checking that
# for a subset, for every pair i < j in sorted order, x_j - x_i >= w_i + w_j
# This is equivalent to x_j - w_j >= x_i + w_i

# We can use a greedy algorithm that goes through points in sorted order and maintains
# the current maximum of (x_i + w_i) for points in the current clique.

# However, to include all points, we need to ensure that when we add a point, 
# its (x_j - w_j) is at least the maximum of (x_i + w_i) for all points in the clique.

# Algorithm:
# 1. Sort by x
# 2. Initialize max_sum = -1 (will store max of x_i + w_i)
# 3. Count = 0
# 4. For each point in sorted order:
#    if x_j - w_j >= max_sum:
#        count += 1
#        max_sum = max(max_sum, x_j + w_j)
# 5. Return count

max_sum = -1
count = 0
for x, w in points:
    if x - w >= max_sum:
        count += 1
        max_sum = max(max_sum, x + w)

print(count)
```

(3,1): 3-1=2 >=5? No

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

The palindrome is a string that can be read the same way from left to right and from right to left. For example, strings "aaaaa", "1221", "bbaabb" are palindromes, however the string "chef" is not a palindrome because if we read it from right to left, we will obtain "fehc" that is not the same as "chef".

We call a string a "double string" if it has an even length and the first half of this string is equal to the second half of this string, for example "abab" is a double string because the first half "ab" is equal to the second half "ab", however the string "abba" is not a double string because the first half "ab" is not equal to the second half "ba". The empty string "" is a double string, and its length is 0.

Chef doesn't like palindromes, however he likes "double strings". He often likes to change the order of letters in some palindrome and sometimes to remove some symbols from it. Now he wonders: if a palindrome of length N is given, what is the maximal possible number of characters in a "double string" 
that can be obtained by removing and changing the order of symbols in it?

------ Input ------ 

Several test cases are given.

The first line of the sample input contains an integer T - the number of test cases.

Then, T lines follow.

Each line consists of a single integer N - the length of a palindrome.

------ Output ------ 

For each test case output a single integer - answer to the problem.

------ Constraints ------ 

$1≤T≤10000$
$1≤N≤1000000000$

----- Sample Input 1 ------ 
2
2
4
----- Sample Output 1 ------ 
2


I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a palindrome of length N
2. We want to create the longest possible "double string" by removing and reordering characters
3. A double string has even length where the first half equals the second half

## Key Insights

1. A palindrome has symmetric properties - if we have a palindrome of length N, the character at position i from the start equals the character at position i from the end.

2. For a double string of length 2k, we need k characters in the first half and k characters in the second half, where the first half equals the second half.

3. Since we can reorder characters freely, we only care about the frequency of each character.

4. In a palindrome of length N, characters come in pairs (except possibly one middle character in odd-length palindromes). For example, in "aba", we have two 'a's and one 'b'.

5. The key insight: in any palindrome, characters appear in pairs. If we have a character with frequency f, we can form pairs of that character.

6. For a double string, we need pairs of characters. Each pair contributes 2 characters. So if we have p pairs of characters, we can form a double string of length 2p.

7. In a palindrome of length N, the maximum number of pairs we can form is ⌊N/2⌋. This is because:
   - In even-length palindromes: all characters form pairs (N/2 pairs)
   - In odd-length palindromes: (N-1)/2 pairs (since one character is in the middle and doesn't form a pair)

8. Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, in any palindrome, characters that appear in symmetric positions form pairs. The total number of symmetric pairs is exactly ⌊N/2⌋.

9. Since we want a double string, we need these pairs to be used in both halves. We can use all ⌊N/2⌋ pairs to form a double string of length 2 × ⌊N/2⌋ = N if N is even, or N-1 if N is odd.

10. For a double string of length 2k, we need exactly k pairs of characters. Since we have ⌊N/2⌋ pairs available, the maximum length of double string is 2 × ⌊N/2⌋.

11. But ⌊N/2⌋ × 2 = N if N is even, and N-1 if N is odd.

So the answer is: if N is even, answer is N; if N is odd, answer is N-1.

Wait, let me verify with the examples:
- N=2: even, answer should be 2 ✓ (matches sample)
- N=4: even, answer should be 4 ✓ (matches sample)

This makes sense because:
- In a palindrome, we have ⌊N/2⌋ character pairs
- For a double string of length 2k, we need k pairs
- Maximum k is ⌊N/2⌋
- Maximum length is 2 × ⌊N/2⌋ = N if N is even, N-1 if N is odd

Actually, 2 × ⌊N/2⌋ = N when N is even, and N-1 when N is odd.

But since we're looking for the maximum possible double string, and we can reorder freely, we can indeed use all character pairs.

Therefore, the answer is simply 2 × ⌊N/2⌋ = N if N is even, N-1 if N is odd.

This simplifies to: if N is even, answer is N; if N is odd, answer is N-1.

We can write this as: N if N is even, N-1 if N is odd, which is equivalent to N - (N % 2).

```python
T = int(input())
for _ in range(T):
    N = int(input())
    print(N - (N % 2))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a palindrome of length N
2. We want to create the longest possible "double string" by removing and rearranging characters
3. A double string has even length and its first half equals its second half

## Key Insights

1. Since we can rearrange characters freely, we only care about the frequency of characters, not their original positions
2. In a palindrome, characters appear in symmetric positions
3. For any character in the original palindrome, it appears the same number of times in both halves

## Analysis of Palindrome Structure

In a palindrome of length N:
- If N is even: characters are symmetric around the center
- If N is odd: there's one character in the middle that appears once in the middle position

Let me think about what characters we can use to form a double string:

A double string of length 2k has first half of length k and second half of length k. Since the first half equals the second half, we need exactly 2k characters total, with k characters in each half.

Since we can rearrange freely, we just need enough total characters. The constraint is that we can only use characters that existed in the original palindrome.

In any palindrome, each character appears an even number of times, except possibly one character in the middle (when N is odd).

Wait, let me reconsider:

In a palindrome, characters appear symmetrically. For position i from the start, there's a corresponding position N-1-i from the end. 

- When N is even: every character appears an even number of times (paired symmetrically)
- When N is odd: all characters except one appear an even number of times, and one character appears an odd number of times (in the middle)

Actually, since we can rearrange freely, we can use all characters. The key constraint is that we need to form a double string with even length.

The maximum double string length we can form is N, but only if N is even. If N is odd, we can form a double string of length N-1.

But wait - can we always form a double string of length N-1 when N is odd?

Yes, because:
- When N is odd, we have N characters total
- We can remove one character (the middle one) and use the remaining N-1 characters
- Since N-1 is even, we can arrange these N-1 characters into a double string of length N-1
- We can pair up the characters in any way to form the first and second halves

When N is even:
- We have N characters, all arranged symmetrically
- We can form a double string of length N
- We just need to group the characters appropriately

## Final Answer

- If N is even: answer is N
- If N is odd: answer is N-1

But let me verify with the examples:
- N=2 (even): answer should be 2 ✓
- N=4 (even): answer should be 4 ✓

This matches the sample output.

## Edge Cases

- N=1 (odd): we can only form a double string of length 0 (since we need even length and can't form length 1)
- N=3 (odd): we have 3 characters, remove one middle character, use 2 characters to form a double string of length 2

Wait, for N=1: we have a palindrome "a". We can only form a double string of even length. The maximum even length we can get is 0 (empty string). So answer should be 0.

For N=1: answer = 1-1 = 0 ✓

So the formula holds.

## Algorithm

For each test case:
- If N is even: output N
- If N is odd: output N-1

This can be simplified to: output N if N is even, else N-1.

This is equivalent to: output N - (N % 2)

```python
T = int(input())
for _ in range(T):
    N = int(input())
    if N % 2 == 0:
        print(N)
    else:
        print(N - 1)
```

The palindrome is a string that can be read the same way from left to right and from right to left. For example, strings "aaaaa", "1221", "bbaabb" are palindromes, however the string "chef" is not a palindrome because if we read it from right to left, we will obtain "fehc" that is not the same as "chef".

We call a string a "double string" if it has an even length and the first half of this string is equal to the second half of this string, for example "abab" is a double string because the first half "ab" is equal to the second half "ab", however the string "abba" is not a double string because the first half "ab" is not equal to the second half "ba". The empty string "" is a double string, and its length is 0.

Chef doesn't like palindromes, however he likes "double strings". He often likes to change the order of letters in some palindrome and sometimes to remove some symbols from it. Now he wonders: if a palindrome of length N is given, what is the maximal possible number of characters in a "double string" 
that can be obtained by removing and changing the order of symbols in it?

The first line of the sample input contains an integer T - the number of test cases.

Then, T lines follow.

Each line consists of a single integer N - the length of a palindrome.

$1≤N≤1000000000$

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a palindrome of length N
2. We want to create the longest possible "double string" by removing and reordering characters
3. A double string has even length where the first half equals the second half

## Key Insights

1. Since we can reorder characters freely, we only care about the frequency of each character
2. A palindrome has a special property: characters appear in pairs, with at most one character appearing an odd number of times (the middle character)

3. In a palindrome of length N, the character frequencies have the following property:
   - If N is odd, one character appears an odd number of times, all others appear even times
   - If N is even, all characters appear even times

4. For a double string of length L (where L is even), we need to form two identical halves. This means we need pairs of characters.

5. The maximum number of characters we can form in a double string depends on how many character pairs we can extract from the original palindrome.

## Analysis

In any palindrome:
- Each character contributes to the total count
- To form a double string, we need to form pairs of characters
- Since we can reorder freely, we can group characters optimally

- If N is even: all characters appear in even frequencies
- If N is odd: one character appears in an odd frequency, others in even frequencies

- If N is even: sum of (freq(i) // 2) = (total length) / 2 = N/2

- N=2: 2 - (2%2) = 2 - 0 = 2 ✓

## Final Answer

For any palindrome of length N, the maximum number of characters in a double string is N if N is even, and N-1 if N is odd. This is simply N - (N % 2).


I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a palindrome of length N
2. We want to create the longest possible "double string" by removing and rearranging characters
3. A double string has even length where the first half equals the second half
4. We need to find the maximum possible length of such a double string

## Key Insights

1. In a palindrome, characters appear in symmetric positions. For a palindrome of length N, if we have a character at position i, we also have it at position (N-1-i).

2. For a double string, we need to form two identical halves. So if we have a character that appears k times in the original palindrome, we can use at most floor(k/2) of them in each half of the double string.

3. Since we're free to rearrange the characters, the question becomes: what's the maximum even length we can make with the given character frequencies?

4. In a palindrome, each character appears an even number of times (except possibly one character in the middle if N is odd). But when we form a double string, we don't care about the original palindrome structure - we just need to use the available characters.

5. Since we need the first half to equal the second half, each character that appears in the first half must appear in the second half too. So for any character, if it appears k times, we can use at most floor(k/2) × 2 = k characters (if k is even) or (k-1) characters (if k is odd).

6. Actually, let's think more carefully: if a character appears k times in the palindrome, we can use it in the double string in pairs. Each pair contributes 2 characters (1 in first half, 1 in second half). So we can use up to 2 × floor(k/2) characters of this type.

7. The total number of characters we can use is the sum of 2 × floor(count/2) for all characters.

8. But since we're free to rearrange, and we want maximum length, we should use as many characters as possible in pairs.

9. In any string, if we have a character that appears k times, we can form floor(k/2) pairs of it. Each pair contributes 2 characters to our double string.

10. So the total length of the double string is 2 × sum(floor(count/2) for all characters).

11. For any character count k, floor(k/2) is the maximum number of pairs we can form.

## Simplification

Since we're dealing with a general palindrome, we need to understand the character frequency distribution.

In a palindrome of length N:
- If N is even: every character appears an even number of times
- If N is odd: one character appears an odd number of times (the middle one), and all others appear even number of times

But for forming a double string, we only care about pairs. Each character contributes 2 × floor(count/2) to the total length.

Since floor(count/2) is the number of pairs we can form for that character, and we can make pairs from any character, the total length is 2 × sum(floor(count/2)).

The key insight: in any string of length N, the sum of floor(count/2) for all characters is at most N//2 (because the remainder when distributing characters into pairs).

Actually, let me reconsider: if we have N characters total, and we want to form the maximum number of pairs (each pair contributes 2 characters to a double string), then the maximum number of pairs is floor(N/2) × something?

Wait, no. Each pair of identical characters contributes 2 characters. But we can't form a pair unless we have two of the same character.

The maximum number of characters we can use in a double string is determined by how many pairs we can form from the available character frequencies.

For any multiset of characters, the maximum number of pairs we can form is limited by the available frequencies.

The answer is simply N, because we can rearrange all characters and form a double string of length N if N is even, or N-1 if N is odd (since we need even length).

But wait - can we form a double string of length N if N is odd? No, because double strings must have even length.

So:
- If N is even, we can potentially use all N characters
- If N is odd, we can use at most N-1 characters (making a double string of length N-1)

But is N-1 always achievable? 

Yes, because in a palindrome of odd length N, we have one character that appears an odd number of times. We can remove that one character (or just use pairs of the other characters), and arrange the remaining N-1 characters (all even counts) into a double string.

Since we can rearrange freely, we can always rearrange any even number of characters into a double string of that length.

Therefore:
- If N is even: answer is N
- If N is odd: answer is N-1

But wait, let's check the examples:
- N=2 (even): answer should be 2 ✓
- N=4 (even): answer should be 4 ✓

Since N=2 and N=4 both give answers of 2 and 4 respectively, this matches.

Actually, let me verify this logic more carefully:
- For any palindrome of length N, we can always form a double string of length N if N is even
- If N is odd, we can remove one character (the one with odd count) and form a double string of length N-1

Is there any constraint that prevents us from achieving N-1 when N is odd? No, because after removing the middle character, we have a string of length N-1 (even) with all characters appearing even times, so we can rearrange it into a double string.

Therefore, the answer is:
- N if N is even
- N-1 if N is odd

This simplifies to N if N even, and N-1 if N odd, which is equivalent to N - (N % 2)

```python
T = int(input())
for _ in range(T):
    N = int(input())
    if N % 2 == 0:
        print(N)
    else:
        print(N - 1)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a palindrome of length N
2. We want to create the longest possible "double string" by removing and reordering characters
3. A double string has even length and the first half equals the second half

## Key Insights

1. Since we can reorder characters freely, we can arrange them optimally
2. A double string of length 2k has first half of length k and second half of length k, where first half = second half
3. For a double string of length 2k, we need exactly 2k characters total
4. Since we can reorder freely, we can form a double string as long as we have enough characters
5. The constraint is that we start with a palindrome

## Analyzing the Palindrome Structure

In a palindrome:
- Characters are symmetric: position i from start equals position i from end
- If N is odd, there's a middle character that appears once
- If N is even, all characters come in pairs

For a palindrome of length N:
- If N is even: each character appears in pairs (so we have N/2 pairs total)
- If N is odd: we have (N-1)/2 pairs plus one middle character that appears once

## Forming Double Strings

For a double string of length 2k:
- We need k characters for the first half
- We need k characters for the second half
- Since the first half equals the second half, we need 2k characters in total

Since we can reorder freely, we can use any characters from the palindrome. The limiting factor is the total number of characters we can use.

### Case 1: N is even
- We have N/2 pairs of characters
- We can form a double string of length 2k where k ≤ N/2
- Maximum k = N/2, so maximum double string length = 2 × (N/2) = N

### Case 2: N is odd  
- We have (N-1)/2 pairs of characters
- We have 1 character that appears once (the middle)
- We can form a double string of length 2k where k ≤ (N-1)/2
- Maximum k = (N-1)/2, so maximum double string length = 2 × ((N-1)/2) = N-1

- For even N: we have N/2 pairs
- For odd N: we have (N-1)/2 pairs and 1 singleton

- For odd N: we have one character (the middle) that appears once - this is odd
- We can't use this odd-count character in a double string because it would make the count odd

So for odd N:
- We have (N-1)/2 pairs, which gives us (N-1)/2 × 2 = N-1 characters
- We can form a double string of length N-1 (using all the paired characters)

For even N:
- We have N/2 pairs, giving us N characters
- We can form a double string of length N

Actually, let me think again. Since we can reorder freely, we can group the characters optimally.

In any palindrome:
- Characters come in pairs (except possibly one middle character)
- For a double string, we need each character to appear an even number of times (since first half = second half)

So the maximum length of a double string is determined by how many characters we can form with even counts.

In a palindrome of length N:
- If N is even: we have N/2 pairs → we can make a double string of length N
- If N is odd: we have (N-1)/2 pairs and one singleton → we can only use the paired characters, giving us N-1 characters → double string of length N-1

Wait, but since each pair gives us two identical characters, we can use each pair to form a double string of length 2k where k is the number of pairs.

- If N is even: N (we can use all characters)
- If N is odd: N-1 (we can't use the middle character since it would make one character odd in count)

- If N is even: N

## Key Insights

1. A palindrome has symmetric properties - characters appear in pairs (except possibly one middle character in odd-length palindromes)

2. Since we can remove and reorder characters freely, we only care about the frequency of characters, not their original positions

3. In a palindrome of length N:
   - If N is even: all characters appear in pairs (N/2 pairs total)
   - If N is odd: (N-1)/2 pairs plus one middle character (which we can ignore since we can't create a palindrome with odd-length double string)

4. For a double string of length 2k, we need exactly k pairs of characters - each character must appear at least twice to form a pair.

5. To maximize the length of the double string, we want to use as many pairs as possible.

## Analysis by Cases

For a palindrome of length N:
- Total number of character pairs available: N // 2
- Since we need a double string of even length, we can only form pairs from these available pairs
- The maximum length of a double string = 2 × (number of character pairs)

- Each character appears in symmetric positions
- For a palindrome of length N, we have exactly N//2 pairs of identical characters (the middle character in odd-length palindromes doesn't contribute to pairs)

In a palindrome of length N:
- If N is even: we have exactly N/2 pairs of identical characters
- If N is odd: we have exactly (N-1)/2 pairs of identical characters

For a double string of length 2k, we need k pairs of characters.

Since we can use any characters from the palindrome, the maximum number of pairs we can form is limited by the total number of character pairs in the original palindrome.

In a palindrome of length N, the total number of character pairs is N // 2 (each pair contributes 2 characters).

Therefore, the maximum length of a double string = 2 × (N // 2) = N.

- For N=4: palindrome has 2 pairs → double string length = 4 ✓

Actually, I think the answer is simply N when N is even, and N-1 when N is odd.

But for N=3 (odd): a palindrome like "aba" has pairs: (a,a) in positions 0,2 and middle 'b'. So we have 1 pair of 'a's.

We can form a double string of length 2 (using the two 'a's). So answer = 2.

For any palindrome, the maximum number of pairs we can use is N // 2.

Therefore, the maximum length of double string = 2 × (N // 2) = N if N even, N-1 if N odd.

But 2 × (N // 2) = N when N is even, and N-1 when N is odd.

However, "N if N is even, N-1 if N is odd" is the same as N // 2 * 2 = N if even, (N-1) if odd.

Actually, 2 * (N // 2) = N when N is even, and N-1 when N is odd.

So for any N, the answer is 2 * (N // 2) = N if N is even, N-1 if N is odd.

But looking at the examples:
- N=2: answer = 2 ✓
- N=4: answer = 4 ✓

Since we can always rearrange to form a double string using all available pairs, and we have N//2 pairs, the maximum double string length is 2 * (N // 2).

Wait, this is actually just N when N is even, and N-1 when N is odd.

But 2 * (N // 2) is exactly this value.

For N=2: 2 * (2//2) = 2 * 1 = 2
For N=4: 2 * (4//2) = 2 * 2 = 4
For N=3: 2 * (3//2) = 2 * 1 = 2

Yes! This matches.

So the answer is simply 2 * (N // 2) = N if N is even, N-1 if N is odd.

But notice that 2 * (N // 2) is equal to N when N is even, and N-1 when N is odd.

Actually, this is just N if N is even, N-1 if N is odd.

But since N//2 * 2 = N when even, and (N-1)//2 * 2 = N-1 when odd.

Yes, the answer is 2 * (N // 2).

## Final Solution

```python
T = int(input())
for _ in range(T):
    N = int(input())
    print(2 * (N // 2))
```

Problem

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

The palindrome is a string that can be read the same way from left to right and from right to left. For example, strings "aaaaa", "1221", "bbaabb" are palindromes, however the string "chef" is not a palindrome because if we read it from right to left, we will obtain "fehc" that is not the same as "chef".

We call a string a "double string" if it has an even length and the first half of this string is equal to the second half of this string, for example "abab" is a double string because the first half "ab" is equal to the second half "ab", however the string "abba" is not a double string because the first half "ab" is not equal to the second half "ba". The empty string "" is a double string, and its length is 0.

Chef doesn't like palindromes, however he likes "double strings". He often likes to change the order of letters in some palindrome and sometimes to remove some symbols from it. Now he wonders: if a palindrome of length N is given, what is the maximal possible number of characters in a "double string"

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a palindrome of length N
2. We want to create the longest "double string" possible by removing and reordering characters
3. A double string has even length and the first half equals the second half (like "abab" where first half "ab" = second half "ab")

## Key Insights

1. Since we can reorder characters freely, we only care about the frequency of each character
2. In a palindrome, characters are arranged symmetrically
3. For any palindrome of length N, we need to analyze how characters can be distributed

## Analysis of Palindromes

In any palindrome:
- If N is odd: one character appears an odd number of times (the middle one), all others appear even times
- If N is even: all characters appear even times

## Double String Requirements

For a double string of length 2k:
- We need two identical halves of length k each
- Each character in the first half must appear in the second half as well
- So we need pairs of characters

## Character Frequency Analysis

Since we can reorder freely:
- In a palindrome of length N, characters come in pairs (except possibly one middle character)
- For a double string, we need characters to appear in pairs

**Case 1: N is even**
- All characters appear an even number of times
- We can use all N characters to form a double string
- Since we need pairs for the two halves, we can form a double string of length N

**Case 2: N is odd**
- One character appears an odd number of times (the middle one)
- All other characters appear even times
- We can use all characters except the middle one (which we can't use to form pairs)
- So we can use N-1 characters
- But N-1 is even, so we can form a double string of length N-1

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, in any palindrome, the frequency pattern is such that:
- For even N: all characters appear even times
- For odd N: one character appears odd times, others appear even times

In a double string, every character must appear in pairs (since the first half and second half need to be identical).

So:
- For even N: we can use all N characters (all even frequencies)
- For odd N: we can only use characters with even frequencies, which gives us N-1 characters

But the double string of length N-1 can be formed since N-1 is even.

Actually, let me think simpler: the maximum length of a double string we can form is the largest even number ≤ N.

For any palindrome of length N, we can always form a double string of length N if N is even, and of length N-1 if N is odd.

This is because:
- When N is even: we have even-length palindrome → all characters appear in pairs → we can form a double string of length N
- When N is odd: we have odd-length palindrome → one character appears odd times → we can only use the even occurrences of characters → total even length is N-1

Therefore, the answer is:
- If N is even: N
- If N is odd: N-1

Which is simply: N if N is even, else N-1

This can be written as: N - (N % 2)

## Verification with Examples

- N=2 (even): answer should be 2 ✓
- N=4 (even): answer should be 4 ✓

## Problem Understanding

2. We want to create the longest possible "double string" by removing and reordering characters
3. A double string has even length where the first half equals the second half

## Key Insights

1. A palindrome has a specific structure: it reads the same forwards and backwards
2. In a palindrome of length N, characters are symmetric - the i-th character from the start equals the i-th character from the end
3. For a double string, we need to form two identical halves

## Analysis of Character Frequencies

In a palindrome:
- If N is odd, there's one middle character that appears once
- If N is even, all characters appear in pairs (symmetrically)

Let me think about what characters we can use to form a double string:

In a double string of length 2k, we need k characters in the first half and k identical characters in the second half. So we need 2k characters total, where the first k characters must be the same as the last k characters.

Since we can reorder and remove characters, we're essentially asking: what's the maximum length of a double string we can form from the character counts available in the palindrome?

## Character Distribution in Palindromes

- Characters appear in symmetric pairs
- If N is odd, we have (N-1)/2 pairs of characters and 1 single character in the middle
- If N is even, we have N/2 pairs of characters

- So maximum double string length is 2 × (total number of character pairs) = 2 × (N-1)/2 = N-1 when N is odd
- If N is even: all characters appear in pairs, so we can use all N characters

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

We have an undirected graph with (N_1+N_2) vertices and M edges.  For i=1,2,\ldots,M, the i-th edge connects vertex a_i and vertex b_i.

The following properties are guaranteed:

- Vertex u and vertex v are connected, for all integers u and v with 1 \leq u,v \leq N_1.
- Vertex u and vertex v are connected, for all integers u and v with N_1+1 \leq u,v \leq N_1+N_2.
- Vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are disconnected.

Consider performing the following operation exactly once:

- choose an integer u with 1 \leq u \leq N_1 and an integer v with N_1+1 \leq v \leq N_1+N_2, and add an edge connecting vertex u and vertex v.

We can show that vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are always connected in the resulting graph; so let d be the minimum length (number of edges) of a path between vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2).  
Find the maximum possible d resulting from adding an appropriate edge to add.

Definition of "connected"

Two vertices u and v of an undirected graph are said to be connected if and only if there is a path between vertex u and vertex v.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N_1 N_2 M

a_1 b_1

\vdots

a_M b_M

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N_1,N_2 \leq 1.5 \times 10^5
- 0 \leq M \leq 3 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq a_i \leq b_i \leq N_1+N_2
- (a_i,b_i) \neq (a_j,b_j) if i \neq j.
- Vertex u and vertex v are connected for all integers u and v such that 1 \leq u,v \leq N_1.
- Vertex u and vertex v are connected for all integers u and v such that N_1+1 \leq u,v \leq N_1+N_2.
- Vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are disconnected.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4 6

1 3

6 7

Sample Output 1

5

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected graph with (N₁ + N₂) vertices and M edges
2. The first N₁ vertices (1 to N₁) form a connected component
3. The last N₂ vertices (N₁+1 to N₁+N₂) form a connected component  
4. Vertex 1 and vertex (N₁+N₂) are initially disconnected
5. We must add exactly one edge between a vertex u (1 ≤ u ≤ N₁) and vertex v (N₁+1 ≤ v ≤ N₁+N₂)
6. After adding this edge, we want to maximize the shortest path distance between vertex 1 and vertex (N₁+N₂)

## Key Insight

When we add an edge between u ∈ [1, N₁] and v ∈ [N₁+1, N₁+N₂], the shortest path from 1 to (N₁+N₂) will be:
- Distance from 1 to u + 1 (for the new edge) + distance from v to (N₁+N₂)

So we want to maximize: d₁(u) + 1 + d₂(v)

Where:
- d₁(u) = shortest distance from vertex 1 to vertex u in the first component
- d₂(v) = shortest distance from vertex v to vertex (N₁+N₂) in the second component

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from input
2. Find shortest distances from vertex 1 to all vertices in the first component (1 to N₁) using BFS
3. Find shortest distances from vertex (N₁+N₂) to all vertices in the second component (N₁+1 to N₁+N₂) using BFS
4. For each possible edge (u, v) where u ∈ [1, N₁] and v ∈ [N₁+1, N₁+N₂]:
   - Calculate path length = dist1[u] + 1 + dist2[v]
5. Return the maximum such path length

## Implementation

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    N1, N2, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
        edges.append((a, b))
    
    # BFS from vertex 1 to find distances to all vertices in first component
    dist1 = [-1] * (N1 + N2 + 1)
    queue = deque([1])
    dist1[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist1[v] == -1:
                dist1[v] = dist1[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # BFS from vertex (N1+N2) to find distances to all vertices in second component
    dist2 = [-1] * (N1 + N2 + 1)
    queue = deque([N1 + N2])
    dist2[N1 + N2] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist2[v] == -1:
                dist2[v] = dist2[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Find maximum path length
    max_distance = 0
    
    # Try all possible edges between first and second components
    for u in range(1, N1 + 1):
        if dist1[u] != -1:  # u is reachable from 1
            for v in range(N1 + 1, N1 + N2 + 1):
                if dist2[v] != -1:  # v is reachable from N1+N2
                    distance = dist1[u] + 1 + dist2[v]
                    max_distance = max(max_distance, distance)
    
    print(max_distance)

solve()
```

## Verification with Sample 1

Input: N₁=3, N₂=4, M=6, edges: (1,2),(2,3),(4,5),(4,6),(1,3),(6,7)

First component (1-3): distances from 1
- dist1[1] = 0
- dist1[2] = 1 (via 1-2)
- dist1[3] = 1 (via 1-3)

Second component (4-7): distances from 7
- dist2[7] = 0
- dist2[6] = 1 (via 6-7)
- dist2[5] = 2 (via 4-5, 4-6-7)
- dist2[4] = 2 (via 4-6-7)

Possible edges and distances:
- (2,5): 1 + 1 + 2 = 4
- (2,6): 1 + 1 + 1 = 3  
- (2,4): 1 + 1 + 2 = 4
- (3,5): 1 + 1 + 2 = 4
- (3,6): 1 + 1 + 1 = 3
- (3,4): 1 + 1 + 2 = 4

Wait, this gives 4, but expected is 5. Let me recheck...

Actually, let me reconsider. The distance from 1 to 3 is actually 1, and from 6 to 7 is 1, from 4 to 5 is 2 (4-6-7) or 4-5 directly?

From 7 to 4: 7-6-4 (distance 2)
From 7 to 5: 7-6-4-5 (distance 3)

So dist2[5] = 3, dist2[4] = 2

For (2,5): 1 + 1 + 3 = 5 ✓

So the maximum is 5, which matches the sample.

The algorithm is correct.

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    N1, N2, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # BFS from vertex 1 to find distances to all vertices in first component
    dist1 = [-1] * (N1 + N2 + 1)
    queue = deque([1])
    dist1[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist1[v] == -1:
                dist1[v] = dist1[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # BFS from vertex (N1+N2) to find distances to all vertices in second component
    dist2 = [-1] * (N1 + N2 + 1)
    queue = deque([N1 + N2])
    dist2[N1 + N2] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist2[v] == -1:
                dist2[v] = dist2[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Find maximum path length
    max_distance = 0
    
    # Try all possible edges between first and second components
    for u in range(1, N1 + 1):
        if dist1[u] != -1:
            for v in range(N1 + 1, N1 + N2 + 1):
                if dist2[v] != -1:
                    distance = dist1[u] + 1 + dist2[v]
                    max_distance = max(max_distance, distance)
    
    print(max_distance)

solve()
```

from collections import deque, defaultdict

def solve():
    N1, N2, M = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Build adjacency list
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(M):
        a, b = map(int, input().split())
        graph[a].append(b)
        graph[b].append(a)
    
    # BFS from vertex 1 to find distances to all vertices in first component
    dist1 = [-1] * (N1 + N2 + 1)
    queue = deque([1])
    dist1[1] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist1[v] == -1:
                dist1[v] = dist1[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # BFS from vertex (N1+N2) to find distances to all vertices in second component
    dist2 = [-1] * (N1 + N2 + 1)
    queue = deque([N1 + N2])
    dist2[N1 + N2] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist2[v] == -1:
                dist2[v] = dist2[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    # Find maximum path length
    max_distance = 0
    
    # Try all possible edges between first and second components
    for u in range(1, N1 + 1):
        if dist1[u] != -1:
            for v in range(N1 + 1, N1 + N2 + 1):
                if dist2[v] != -1:
                    distance = dist1[u] + 1 + dist2[v]
                    max_distance = max(max_distance, distance)
    
    print(max_distance)

solve()

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

We have an undirected graph with (N_1+N_2) vertices and M edges.  For i=1,2,\ldots,M, the i-th edge connects vertex a_i and vertex b_i.

The following properties are guaranteed:

- Vertex u and vertex v are connected, for all integers u and v with 1 \leq u,v \leq N_1.
- Vertex u and vertex v are connected, for all integers u and v with N_1+1 \leq u,v \leq N_1+N_2.
- Vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are disconnected.

Consider performing the following operation exactly once:

- choose an integer u with 1 \leq u \leq N_1 and an integer v with N_1+1 \leq v \leq N_1+N_2, and add an edge connecting vertex u and vertex v.

We can show that vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are always connected in the resulting graph; so let d be the minimum length (number of edges) of a path between vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2).  
Find the maximum possible d resulting from adding an appropriate edge to add.

Definition of "connected"

Two vertices u and v of an undirected graph are said to be connected if and only if there is a path between vertex u and vertex v.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N_1 N_2 M

a_1 b_1

\vdots

a_M b_M

Output

Print the answer.

- (a_i,b_i) \neq (a_j,b_j) if i \neq j.
- Vertex u and vertex v are connected for all integers u and v such that 1 \leq u,v \leq N_1.
- Vertex u and vertex v are connected for all integers u and v such that N_1+1 \leq u,v \leq N_1+N_2.
- Vertex 1 and vertex (N_1+N_2) are disconnected.
- All input values are integers.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected graph with (N₁ + N₂) vertices and M edges
2. The first N₁ vertices (1 to N₁) form a connected component
3. The last N₂ vertices (N₁+1 to N₁+N₂) form a connected component
4. Vertex 1 and vertex (N₁+N₂) are initially disconnected
5. We must add exactly one edge between a vertex u (1 ≤ u ≤ N₁) and a vertex v (N₁+1 ≤ v ≤ N₁+N₂)
6. After adding this edge, vertex 1 and vertex (N₁+N₂) become connected
7. We want to maximize the shortest path distance d between vertex 1 and vertex (N₁+N₂) after adding the edge

## Key Insight

When we add an edge between u (in first component) and v (in second component), the shortest path from 1 to (N₁+N₂) will be:
- Distance from 1 to u + 1 (for the new edge) + Distance from v to (N₁+N₂)

To maximize this distance, we need to:
- Maximize the distance from 1 to u in the first component
- Maximize the distance from v to (N₁+N₂) in the second component
- The total distance = max_dist_from_1_to_u + 1 + max_dist_from_v_to_end

## Solution Steps

1. Build the graph from input
2. Find the shortest distance from vertex 1 to all vertices in the first component (using BFS)
3. Find the shortest distance from vertex (N₁+N₂) to all vertices in the second component (using BFS)
4. For each vertex u in first component and v in second component, calculate the path length
5. Return the maximum path length

## Implementation

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def bfs_distances(graph, start, n):
    """Return distances from start to all vertices in a graph"""
    n_vertices = n
    distances = [-1] * (n_vertices + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    distances[start] = 0
    
    while queue:
        node = queue.popleft()
        for neighbor in graph[node]:
            if distances[neighbor] == -1:
                distances[neighbor] = distances[node] + 1
                queue.append(neighbor)
    
    return distances

# Read input
n1, n2, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = defaultdict(list)
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Find distances from vertex 1 to all vertices in first component (1 to n1)
dist_from_1 = bfs_distances(graph, 1, n1 + n2)

# Find distances from vertex (n1+n2) to all vertices in second component (n1+1 to n1+n2)
dist_from_end = bfs_distances(graph, n1 + n2, n1 + n2)

# Find maximum distance from 1 to any vertex in first component
max_dist_1_to_u = 0
for u in range(1, n1 + 1):
    if dist_from_1[u] > max_dist_1_to_u:
        max_dist_1_to_u = dist_from_1[u]

# Find maximum distance from (n1+n2) to any vertex in second component
max_dist_v_to_end = 0
for v in range(n1 + 1, n1 + n2 + 1):
    if dist_from_end[v] > max_dist_v_to_end:
        max_dist_v_to_end = dist_from_end[v]

# The maximum possible distance is the sum of these two maxima plus 1 (for the new edge)
result = max_dist_1_to_u + 1 + max_dist_v_to_end

print(result)
```

```python
    return distances
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an undirected graph with N₁ + N₂ vertices and M edges
2. The first N₁ vertices (1 to N₁) form a connected component
3. The last N₂ vertices (N₁+1 to N₁+N₂) form a connected component
4. Vertex 1 and vertex (N₁+N₂) are initially disconnected
5. We must add exactly one edge between a vertex u in [1, N₁] and a vertex v in [N₁+1, N₁+N₂]
6. After adding this edge, we want to maximize the shortest path distance between vertex 1 and vertex (N₁+N₂)

## Key Insights

1. After adding an edge between u and v, the shortest path from 1 to (N₁+N₂) will be:
   - Distance from 1 to u (in first component) + 1 (for the new edge) + Distance from v to (N₁+N₂) (in second component)

2. To maximize this distance, we need to:
   - Find the maximum distance from any vertex in the first component to vertex 1
   - Find the maximum distance from any vertex in the second component to vertex (N₁+N₂)
   - The maximum possible path length will be: (max distance from 1 to any vertex in first component) + 1 + (max distance from any vertex in second component to (N₁+N₂))

3. However, we need to be careful - the distance from 1 to a vertex u is the shortest path, so we need the actual distances.

## Algorithm Steps

1. Build the graph from input
2. Use BFS from vertex 1 to compute distances to all vertices in the first N₁ vertices
3. Use BFS from vertex (N₁+N₂) to compute distances to all vertices in the last N₂ vertices
4. Find the maximum distance from 1 to any vertex in first component
5. Find the maximum distance from (N₁+N₂) to any vertex in second component
6. The answer is: max_dist_from_1 + 1 + max_dist_from_N1_N2

Wait, actually I need to reconsider. The distance from 1 to any vertex u (where u ≤ N₁) is what we need, and the distance from (N₁+N₂) to any vertex v (where v ≥ N₁+1) is what we need. The path would be: 1 → u → v → (N₁+N₂), with length = dist(1,u) + 1 + dist(v, N₁+N₂).

So we want to maximize dist(1,u) + dist(v, N₁+N₂) + 1.

Therefore:
- Find max_dist1 = maximum of dist(1,u) for all u in [1, N₁]
- Find max_dist2 = maximum of dist(N₁+N₂, v) for all v in [N₁+1, N₁+N₂]
- Answer = max_dist1 + max_dist2 + 1

## Implementation

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def bfs(start, n, graph):
    """Return distances from start vertex to all vertices in 1 to n"""
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    dist[start] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist[v] == -1:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    return dist

# Read input
n1, n2, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = defaultdict(list)
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Compute distances from vertex 1 to all vertices in first component
dist_from_1 = bfs(1, n1 + n2, graph)

# Compute distances from vertex (n1+n2) to all vertices in second component
dist_from_end = bfs(n1 + n2, n1 + n2, graph)

# Find maximum distance from vertex 1 to any vertex in first component (1 to n1)
max_dist_from_1 = 0
for u in range(1, n1 + 1):
    if dist_from_1[u] > max_dist_from_1:
        max_dist_from_1 = dist_from_1[u]

# Find maximum distance from vertex (n1+n2) to any vertex in second component (n1+1 to n1+n2)
max_dist_from_end = 0
for v in range(n1 + 1, n1 + n2 + 1):
    if dist_from_end[v] > max_dist_from_end:
        max_dist_from_end = dist_from_end[v]

# Answer is maximum path length: from 1 to u to v to (n1+n2)
answer = max_dist_from_1 + max_dist_from_end + 1
print(answer)
```

```python
from collections import deque, defaultdict

def bfs(start, n, graph):
    """Return distances from start vertex to all vertices in 1 to n"""
    dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
    queue = deque([start])
    dist[start] = 0
    
    while queue:
        u = queue.popleft()
        for v in graph[u]:
            if dist[v] == -1:
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1
                queue.append(v)
    
    return dist

# Read input
n1, n2, m = map(int, input().split())

# Build graph
graph = defaultdict(list)
for _ in range(m):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    graph[a].append(b)
    graph[b].append(a)

# Compute distances from vertex 1 to all vertices in first component
dist_from_1 = bfs(1, n1 + n2, graph)

# Compute distances from vertex (n1+n2) to all vertices in second component
dist_from_end = bfs(n1 + n2, n1 + n2, graph)

# Find maximum distance from vertex 1 to any vertex in first component (1 to n1)
max_dist_from_1 = 0
for u in range(1, n1 + 1):
    if dist_from_1[u] > max_dist_from_1:
        max_dist_from_1 = dist_from_1[u]

# Find maximum distance from vertex (n1+n2) to any vertex in second component (n1+1 to n1+n2)
max_dist_from_end = 0
for v in range(n1 + 1, n1 + n2 + 1):
    if dist_from_end[v] > max_dist_from_end:
        max_dist_from_end = dist_from_end[v]

# Answer is maximum path length: from 1 to u to v to (n1+n2)
answer = max_dist_from_1 + max_dist_from_end + 1
print(answer)
```
